# Fox News

For other uses, see [Fox News (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_News_\(disambiguation\)).

The **Fox News Channel** (**FNC**), often referred to as **Fox News**, is an American multinational [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States) news and [political commentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_commentary) television channel and website based in [New York City](645042.md).[^cite_note-3][^cite_note-4] Owned by the **Fox News Media** subsidiary of [Fox Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_Corporation),[^cite_note-5] as of 2022, it is the most-watched cable news network in the United States,[^cite_note-6][^cite_note-7] and as of 2023, it generates approximately 70% of its parent company's pre-tax profit.[^cite_note-8] The channel broadcasts primarily from studios at [1211 Avenue of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1211_Avenue_of_the_Americas) in [Midtown Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midtown_Manhattan). Fox News provides service to 86 countries and territories,[^cite_note-9] with international broadcasts featuring Fox Extra segments during advertising breaks.[^cite_note-10]

The channel was created by Australian-born American media mogul [Rupert Murdoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Murdoch) in 1996 to appeal to a conservative audience, hiring former [Republican](32070.md) media consultant and [CNBC](223441.md) executive [Roger Ailes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Ailes) as its founding CEO.[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12] It launched on October 7, 1996 to 17 million cable subscribers.[^cite_note-King-13] Fox News grew during the late 1990s and 2000s to become the dominant [United States cable news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_cable_news) subscription network.[^cite_note-14] By September 2018, 87 million U.S. households (91% of television subscribers) could receive Fox News.[^cite_note-15] In 2019, it was the top-rated cable network, averaging 2.5 million viewers in prime time.[^cite_note-16][^cite_note-17][^cite_note-18] Murdoch, the executive chairman since 2016,[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20] said in 2023 that he would step down and hand responsibilities to his son, [Lachlan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lachlan_Murdoch).[^cite_note-Darcy-21] [Suzanne Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzanne_Scott) has been the CEO since 2018.[^cite_note-22]

Fox News has been criticized for [biased and false reporting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_News_controversies#Allegations_of_bias) in favor of the Republican Party, its politicians, and conservative causes,[^cite_note-Jamieson-2010-23][^cite_note-Skocpol-2016-24][^cite_note-Folkenflick_2023-25][^cite_note-Benkler_10182018-26] while portraying the [Democratic Party](5043544.md) in a negative light.[^cite_note-Grossman-2016-27][^cite_note-Bard_2017-28] Some researchers have argued that the channel is damaging to the integrity of news overall,[^cite_note-Collings2010-29][^cite_note-McCollumHebert2014-30] and acts as the *de facto* broadcasting arm of the Republican Party.[^cite_note-Grossman-2016-27][^cite_note-Martin-2017-31][^cite_note-Clinton-2014-32][^cite_note-Schroeder-2015-33] Since its formation, the channel has politically shifted further rightwards over time,[^cite_note-Berry_2014-34][^cite_note-Gabbatt_12192021-35][^cite_note-Rutenberg_09082025-36] and by 2016 became solidly [pro-Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-Trump).[^cite_note-Kludt-2018-37][^cite_note-Schwartz-2017b-38][^cite_note-Ferguson_2021-39][^cite_note-Robertson_08192025-40][^cite_note-Barr_08192025-41]

The channel has knowingly endorsed false conspiracy theories to promote Republican and conservative causes.[^cite_note-Darcy_2024-42][^cite_note-\:0-43] These include, but are not limited to, false claims regarding [fraud with Dominion voting machines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_Voting_Systems_v._Fox_News_Network) during their reporting on the [2020 presidential election](48410011.md),[^cite_note-787m-44][^cite_note-crystal-45] [climate change denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_denial),[^cite_note-Climate_Denial-46] and [COVID-19 misinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_News_controversies#Coverage_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic).[^cite_note-Motta_2020-47][^cite_note-Darcy_2020-48][^cite_note-Rieger_2020-49] It has also been involved in [multiple controversies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_News_controversies), including accusations of permitting [sexual harassment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_harassment) and [racial discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_discrimination) by on-air hosts, executives, and employees,[^cite_note-Guthrie_2017-50][^cite_note-Steel_2017-51][^cite_note-Ember_2017-52] ultimately paying out millions of dollars in legal settlements.[^cite_note-Chappell_2016-53]

## History

Main article: [History of Fox News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Fox_News)

In May 1985, Australian publisher Rupert Murdoch announced that he and American [industrialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialist) and philanthropist [Marvin Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marvin_Davis) intended to develop "a network of [independent stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_station_\(North_America\)) as a fourth marketing force" to directly compete with [CBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS), [NBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC), and [ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Broadcasting_Company) through the purchase of six television stations owned by [Metromedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metromedia).[^cite_note-54] In July 1985, [20th Century Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_Century_Fox) announced Murdoch had completed his purchase of 50% of [Fox Filmed Entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_Entertainment_Group), the parent company of 20th Century Fox Film Corporation.[^cite_note-55]

Subsequently, and prior to founding FNC, Murdoch had gained experience in the 24-hour news business when [News Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/News_Corporation)'s [BSkyB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BSkyB) subsidiary began Europe's first 24-hour news channel ([Sky News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sky_News)) in the United Kingdom in 1989.[^cite_note-56] With the success of his efforts establishing Fox as a TV network in the United States,[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58] experience gained from Sky News and the turnaround of 20th Century Fox, Murdoch announced on January 30, 1996, that News Corp. would launch a 24-hour news channel on cable and satellite systems in the United States as part of a News Corp. "worldwide platform" for Fox programming: "The appetite for news – particularly news that explains to people how it affects them – is expanding enormously".[^cite_note-59]

In February 1996, after former U.S. Republican Party political strategist and NBC executive[^cite_note-60] [Roger Ailes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Ailes) left cable television channel [America's Talking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America's_Talking) (now [MS NOW](159846.md)), Murdoch asked him to start Fox News Channel. Ailes demanded five months of 14-hour workdays and several weeks of rehearsal shows before its launch on October 7, 1996.[^cite_note-FoxCorporateInfo-2]

At its debut, 17 million households were able to watch FNC;[^cite_note-King-13] however, it was absent from the largest U.S. media markets of New York City and Los Angeles. Rolling news coverage during the day consisted of 20-minute single-topic shows such as *Fox on Crime* or *Fox on Politics*, surrounded by news headlines. Interviews featured facts at the bottom of the screen about the topic or the guest. The flagship newscast at the time was *The Schneider Report*, with [Mike Schneider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Schneider_\(news_anchor\))'s fast-paced delivery of the news. During the evening, Fox featured opinion shows: *The O'Reilly Report* (later *[The O'Reilly Factor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_O'Reilly_Factor)*), *The Crier Report* (hosted by [Catherine Crier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_Crier)) and *[Hannity & Colmes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hannity_&_Colmes)*. From the beginning, FNC has placed heavy emphasis on visual presentation. Graphics were designed to be colorful and gain attention; this helped the viewer to grasp the main points of what was being said, even if they could not hear the host (with on-screen text summarizing the position of the interviewer or speaker, and "bullet points" when a host was delivering commentary). Fox News also created the "Fox News Alert", which interrupted its regular programming when a [breaking news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breaking_news) story occurred.[^cite_note-61]



Fox News Studios in 2009

To accelerate its adoption by cable providers, Fox News paid systems up to \$11 per subscriber to distribute the channel.[^cite_note-62] This contrasted with the normal practice, in which cable operators paid stations carriage fees for programming. When [Time Warner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_Warner) bought [Ted Turner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Turner)'s [Turner Broadcasting System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turner_Broadcasting_System), a federal [antitrust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antitrust) [consent decree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consent_decree) required Time Warner to carry a second all-news channel in addition to its own [CNN](62028.md) on its [cable systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_Warner_Cable). Time Warner selected MSNBC as the secondary news channel, not Fox News. Fox News claimed this violated an agreement (to carry Fox News). Citing its agreement to keep its U.S. headquarters and a large studio in New York City, News Corporation enlisted the help of Mayor [Rudolph Giuliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolph_Giuliani)'s administration to pressure Time Warner Cable (one of the city's two cable providers) to transmit Fox News on a city-owned channel.[^cite_note-63] City officials threatened to take action affecting Time Warner's cable franchises in the city.[^cite_note-64]

In 2001, during the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md), Fox News was the first news organization to run a [news ticker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/News_ticker) on the bottom of the screen to keep up with the flow of information that day. The ticker has remained, and has proven popular with viewers.[^cite_note-The_Fox_News_Ticker-65] In January 2002, Fox News surpassed CNN in ratings for the first time.[^cite_note-66]

Accelerating in the 2000s, the role of conservative media and Fox News led to it being trusted by the Republican Party's base over that of traditional conservative elites, and partly led to Donald Trump's victory in the Republican primaries against the wishes of a very weak party establishment and traditional power brokers.[^cite_note-Gidron-2019-67]<sup>: 27–28 </sup> Fox News subsequently became solidly pro-Trump, and cultivated deep ties between itself and the government.[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69] For his first term, nearly 20 current and former Fox News hosts received administrative and cabinet-level positions in his administration, and his second term also featured 23 current and former Fox News hosts appointed and nominated.[^cite_note-70]

In 2023, *The Economist* reported that Murdoch had "ditched a plan" to remerge News Corporation with Fox because it "faced resistance from News Corp investors unhappy at the prospect of being lumped together with Fox News, which they consider a toxic brand."[^cite_note-71] Later that year, Murdoch said he would step down and that his son Lachlan would take over both Fox Corporation and News Corp,[^cite_note-Darcy-21] although [the succession was disputed legally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succession_of_Rupert_Murdoch). In September 2025, Lachlan Murdoch secured control of Fox News, the *[New York Post](102227.md)* and *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* in a \$3.3 billion deal as part of a renegotiated trust. The new trust and Lachlan's control was described as ensuring the channel's conservative slant until its expiration in 2050.[^cite_note-Rutenberg_09082025-36]

Further information: [Media bias in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_bias_in_the_United_States)

Fox News has been identified as practicing [biased and false reporting](18932.md) in favor of the Republican Party, its politicians, and conservative causes,[^cite_note-Jamieson-2010-23][^cite_note-Skocpol-2016-24][^cite_note-Folkenflick_2023-25][^cite_note-Benkler_10182018-26] while portraying the Democratic Party in a negative light.[^cite_note-Grossman-2016-27][^cite_note-Bard_2017-28] Fox News has been characterized by critics, commentators, and researchers as an [advocacy news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advocacy_journalism) organization[^cite_note-Advocacy-86] and as damaging to the integrity of news overall.[^cite_note-Collings2010-29][^cite_note-McCollumHebert2014-30] It has been criticized for sharing propaganda.[^cite_note-Propaganda-104] The network is pro-Trump.[^cite_note-Kludt-2018-37][^cite_note-Schwartz-2017b-38][^cite_note-Benkler_08232020-105][^cite_note-Benkler_10182018-26][^cite_note-Ferguson_2021-39] During and after the [2020 presidential election](48410011.md), its primetime hosts promoted Trump and the Republican Party,[^cite_note-Robertson_08192025-40] and host [Jeanine Pirro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeanine_Pirro) was in communication with the chair of the [Republican National Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_National_Committee).[^cite_note-Barr_08192025-41]

By 2017, a growing number of studies and academic literature found Fox's prime-time programming engaging in rhetorical and nonfactual themes similar to propaganda and not journalism or persuasion.[^cite_note-Bard_2017-28] Academic studies have argued that it has played a major role in boosting Republican turnout in American elections and that its role in American politics has been underestimated by political and communications scholars.[^cite_note-Yglesias_10022018-89] Fox has been described as operating in an information silo where its audience views other media sources as "too liberal", and thus rely on Fox and no other forms of news media.[^cite_note-Austin_2021-106][^cite_note-107] Researchers and commentators have compared conservative Fox News as similar in purpose to liberal MSNBC,[^cite_note-Atkins_02152025-75][^cite_note-Kumkar_2018-80][^cite_note-Milani_2022-81][^cite_note-Schumacher-Matos_2025-83] but that "the proportion of Fox News statements that are mostly false or worse is almost 50 percent higher than for MSNBC, and more than twice that of CNN".[^cite_note-Benkler_10182018-26][^cite_note-Kumkar_2018-80]

Its news coverage has gradually shifted further rightwards over time.[^cite_note-Berry_2014-34][^cite_note-Gabbatt_12192021-35][^cite_note-Rutenberg_09082025-36] Fox's most popular programs such as *[Hannity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hannity)* and *[Tucker Carlson Tonight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucker_Carlson_Tonight)* do not make any claims to be accurate or [fact-checked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fact-checked), and have little to no distinction between news and commentary.[^cite_note-Austin_2021-106] Media analyst [Brian Stelter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Stelter), who has [written](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoax_\(book\)) [extensively](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Network_of_Lies_\(book\)) about the network, observed in 2021 that in more recent years it had adjusted its programming to present "less news on the air and more opinions-about-the-news" throughout the day, on concerns it was losing viewers to more conservative competitors that were presenting such content.[^cite_note-108]

## Outlets

Fox News airport newsstand

FNC airport [newsstand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newsagent) at [Minneapolis-Saint Paul International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minneapolis-Saint_Paul_International_Airport)

Television news studio

FNC's [newsroom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newsroom), November 15, 2007

FNC maintains an archive of most of its programs. This archive also includes [Movietone News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movietone_News) series of newsreels from its now Disney-owned namesake movie studio, [20th Century Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_Century_Studios). Licensing for the Fox News archive is handled by ITN Source, the archiving division of [ITN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITN).[^cite_note-109]

### Television

Main article: [List of programs broadcast by Fox News Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_programs_broadcast_by_Fox_News_Channel)

FNC presents a variety of programming, with up to 15 hours of live broadcasting per day in addition to programming and content for the Fox Broadcasting Company. Most programs are broadcast from Fox News headquarters in New York City (at [1211 Avenue of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1211_Avenue_of_the_Americas)), in its streetside studio on [Sixth Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_Avenue) in the west wing of [Rockefeller Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockefeller_Center), sharing its headquarters with sister channel Fox Business Network. Fox News Channel has eight studios at its New York City headquarters that are used for its and Fox Business' programming: Studio B (used for Fox Business programming), Studio D (which has an area for studio audiences; no longer in current use), Studio E (used for *[Gutfeld!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gutfeld!)* and *[The Journal Editorial Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Journal_Editorial_Report)*), Studio F (used for *[The Story with Martha MacCallum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Story_with_Martha_MacCallum)*, *[The Five](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Five_\(talk_show\))*, Fox Democracy 2020, *[Fox & Friends](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_&_Friends)*, *[Outnumbered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outnumbered_\(U.S._TV_program\))*, *The Faulkner Focus*, and *Fox News Primetime*), Studio G (which houses Fox Business shows, *[The Fox Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fox_Report)*, *[Your World with Neil Cavuto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Your_World_with_Neil_Cavuto)*, and *[Cavuto Live](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavuto_Live)*), Studio H (Fox News Deck used for breaking news coverage, no longer in current use), Studio J (used for *[America's Newsroom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America's_Newsroom)*, *[Hannity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hannity)*, *[Fox News Live](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_News_Live)*, *[Fox & Friends First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_&_Friends_First)*, and *[Sunday Morning Futures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunday_Morning_Futures_With_Maria_Bartiromo)*) Starting in [2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_NFL_season), *[Thursday Night Football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thursday_Night_Football)* had its pregame show, *[Fox NFL Thursday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_NFL_Sunday)*, originating from Studio F. Another Fox Sports program, *First Things First*, also broadcasts from Studio E.

Other such programs (such as *[Special Report with Bret Baier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Report_with_Bret_Baier)*, *[The Ingraham Angle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ingraham_Angle)*, *[Fox News @ Night](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_News_@_Night)*, *[Media Buzz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_Buzz)*, and editions of *Fox News Live* not broadcast from the New York City studios) are broadcast from Fox News's Washington, D.C. studios, located on [Capitol Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitol_Hill) across from [Washington Union Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Union_Station) in a secured building shared by a number of other television networks, which includes [NBC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC_News) and [C-SPAN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-SPAN). *[The Next Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Next_Revolution)* is broadcast from Fox News' Los Angeles bureau studio, which is also used for news updates coming from Los Angeles. *[Life, Liberty & Levin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life,_Liberty_&_Levin)* is done from Levin's personal studio in [Virginia](32432.md). Audio simulcasts of the channel are aired on [SiriusXM Satellite Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SiriusXM_Satellite_Radio).

In an October 11, 2009, in a *[New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times)* article, Fox said its hard-news programming runs from "9 AM to 4 PM and 6 to 8 PM on weekdays". However, it makes no such claims for its other broadcasts, which primarily consist of editorial journalism and commentary.[^cite_note-110]

Fox News Channel began broadcasting in the [720p](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/720p) resolution format on May 1, 2008.[^cite_note-multichannel1-111] This format is available on all major cable and satellite providers.

Fox News Media produces *[Fox News Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_News_Sunday)*, which airs on Fox Broadcasting and re-airs on the Fox News Channel. Fox News also produces occasional special event coverage that is broadcast on Fox Business.

### Radio

Main article: [Fox News Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_News_Radio)

With the growth of the FNC, the company introduced a radio division, [Fox News Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_News_Radio), in 2003.[^cite_note-112] [Syndicated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syndicated_radio_show) throughout the United States, the division provides short newscasts and talk radio programs featuring personalities from the television and radio divisions. In 2006, the company also introduced [Fox News Talk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_News_Talk), a [satellite radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_radio) station featuring programs syndicated by (and featuring) Fox News personalities.

### Online

Introduced in December 1995,[^cite_note-113] the Fox News website features news articles and videos about national and international news. Content on the website is divided into politics, media, U.S., and business. Fox News' articles are based on the network's broadcasts, reports from Fox affiliates and articles produced by other news agencies, such as the [Associated Press](18935732.md). Articles are usually accompanied by a video related to the article.[^cite_note-NewsGuard-114] Fox News Latino is the version aimed at a [Hispanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans) audience, although presented almost entirely in English, with a Spanish section.[^cite_note-115]

According to [NewsGuard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NewsGuard), "Much of FoxNews.com's content, particularly articles produced by beat reporters and broadcasts produced by network correspondents, is accurate and well-sourced ... However, FoxNews.com has regularly advanced false and misleading claims on topics including the [Jan. 6, 2021, attack on the U.S. Capitol](66307490.md), the [Russo-Ukrainian War](42085878.md), [COVID-19](63030231.md), and [U.S. elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._elections)".[^cite_note-NewsGuard-114]

In September 2008, FNC joined other channels in introducing a live streaming segment to its website: *The Strategy Room*, designed to appeal to older viewers. It airs weekdays from 9 AM to 5 PM and takes the form of an informal discussion, with running commentary on the news. Regular discussion programs include *Business Hour*, *News With a View* and *God Talk*.[^cite_note-116] In March 2009, *The Fox Nation* was launched as a website intended to encourage readers to post articles commenting on the news.[^cite_note-117] Fox News Mobile is the portion of the FNC website dedicated to streaming news clips formatted for video-enabled mobile phones.[^cite_note-118]

### Fox Nation

Main article: [Fox Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_Nation)

In 2018, Fox News announced that it would launch a [subscription video on demand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_on_demand) service known as [Fox Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_Nation). It serves as a companion service to FNC, carrying original and acquired talk, documentary, and reality programming designed to appeal to Fox News viewers. Some of its original programs feature Fox News personalities and contributors.*[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-Network-120][^cite_note-Malone-2021-121][^cite_note-122]*

## Ratings and reception

In 2003, Fox News saw a large ratings jump during the early stages of the [U.S. invasion of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._invasion_of_Iraq). At the height of the conflict, according to some reports, Fox News had as much as a 300% increase in viewership (averaging 3.3 million viewers daily).[^cite_note-123] In 2004, Fox News' ratings for its broadcast of the [Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Republican_National_Convention) exceeded those of the three major broadcast networks. During President [George W. Bush](3414021.md)'s address, Fox News attracted 7.3 million viewers nationally; NBC, ABC, and CBS had a viewership of 5.9 million, 5.1 million, and 5.0 million respectively.

Between late 2005 and early 2006, Fox News saw a brief decline in ratings. One was in the second quarter of 2006, when it lost viewers for every prime-time program compared with the previous quarter. The audience for *[Special Report with Brit Hume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Report_with_Brit_Hume)*, for example, dropped 19%. Several weeks later, in the wake of the [2006 North Korean missile test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_North_Korean_missile_test) and the [2006 Lebanon War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Lebanon_War), Fox saw a surge in viewership and remained the top-rated cable news channel.[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-125] Fox produced eight of the top ten most-watched nightly cable news shows, with *The O'Reilly Factor* and *[Hannity & Colmes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hannity_&_Colmes)* finishing first and second respectively.[^cite_note-126]

FNC ranked No. 8 in viewership among all cable channels in 2006, and No. 7 in 2007.[^cite_note-127] The channel ranked number one during the week of Barack Obama's election (November 3–9) in 2008, and reached the top spot again in January 2010 (during the week of the [special Senate election in Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_special_election_in_Massachusetts,_2010)).[^cite_note-128] Comparing Fox to its 24-hour-news-channel competitors in May 2010, the channel drew an average daily prime-time audience of 1.8 million viewers (versus 747,000 for MSNBC and 595,000 for CNN).[^cite_note-129]

In September 2009, the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) published a report on the public view of national news organizations. In the report, 72% of polled Republican Fox viewers rated the channel as "favorable", while 43% of polled Democratic viewers and 55% of all polled viewers shared that opinion. However, Fox was given the highest "unfavorable" rating of all national outlets studied (25% of all polled viewers). The report went on to say that "partisan differences in views of Fox News have increased substantially since 2007".[^cite_note-130] A January 2020 Pew Research Center study found that 43% of all American adults trusted Fox News, including 65% of Republicans and people who lean Republican, while 61% of Democrats and people who lean Democratic distrusted Fox News.[^cite_note-131]

A Public Policy Polling poll concluded in 2013 that positive perceptions of FNC had declined from 2010. 41% of polled voters said they trust it, down from 49% in 2010, while 46% said they distrust it, up from 37% in 2010. It was also called the "most trusted" network by 34% of those polled, more than had said the same of any other network.[^cite_note-132]



Then-Fox anchor [Megyn Kelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megyn_Kelly) covering the [2012 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Democratic_National_Convention)

On the night of October 22, 2012, Fox set a record for its highest-rated [telecast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadcasting), with 11.5 million viewers for the third [U.S. presidential debate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_election_debates,_2012).[^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134] In prime time the week before, Fox averaged almost 3.7 million viewers with a total day average of 1.66 million viewers.[^cite_note-135]

In prime time and total day ratings for the week of April 15 to 21, 2013, Fox News, propelled by its coverage of the [Boston Marathon bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Marathon_bombing), was the highest-ranked network on U.S. cable television, for the first time since August 2005, when [Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina) hit the [Gulf Coast of the United States](11969.md).[^cite_note-136] January 2014 marked Fox News's 145th consecutive month as the highest-rated cable news channel. During that month, Fox News beat CNN and MSNBC combined in overall viewers in both prime time hours and the total day.[^cite_note-137] In the third quarter of 2014, the network was the most-watched cable channel during prime time hours.[^cite_note-138] During the final week of the campaign for the [United States elections, 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_elections,_2014), Fox News had the highest ratings of any cable channel, news or otherwise. On election night itself, Fox News' coverage had higher ratings than that of any of the other five cable or network news sources among viewers between 25 and 54 years of age.[^cite_note-139] The network hosted the first prime-time GOP candidates' forum of the 2016 campaign on August 6. The debate reached a record-breaking 24 million viewers, by far the largest audience for any cable news event.[^cite_note-140]

A 2017 study by the [Berkman Klein Center for Internet & Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berkman_Klein_Center_for_Internet_&_Society) at [Harvard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_University) found that Fox News was the third most-shared source among supporters of [Donald Trump](4848272.md) on [Twitter](9988187.md) during the [2016 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election), behind [*The Hill*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hill_\(newspaper\)) and *[Breitbart News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breitbart_News)*.[^cite_note-141][^cite_note-142]

In 2018, Fox News was rated by Nielsen as America's most watched cable network, averaging a record 2.4 million viewers in prime time and total day during the period of January 1 to December 30, 2018.[^cite_note-143] In an October 2018 [Simmons Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simmons_Research) survey of the trust in 38 news organizations, Fox News was ranked roughly in the center, with 44.7% of surveyed Americans saying they trusted it.[^cite_note-144]

The COVID-19 pandemic led to increased viewership for all cable news networks. For the first calendar quarter of 2020 (January 1 – March 31), Fox News had their highest-rated quarter in the network's history, with Nielsen showing a prime time average total audience of 3.387 million viewers. [Sean Hannity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sean_Hannity)'s program, *Hannity*, weeknights at 9 pm ET was the top-rated show in cable news for the quarter averaging 4.2 million viewers, a figure that not only beat out all of its cable news competition but also placed it ahead of network competition in the same time slot. Fox ended the quarter with the top five shows in prime time, with Fox's *[Tucker Carlson Tonight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucker_Carlson_Tonight)* finishing the quarter in second overall with an average audience of 4.2 million viewers, followed by *[The Five](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Five_\(talk_show\))*, *[The Ingraham Angle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ingraham_Angle)*, and *[Special Report with Bret Baier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Report_with_Bret_Baier)*. *[The Rachel Maddow Show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Rachel_Maddow_Show)* was the highest non-Fox show on cable, coming in sixth place. Finishing the quarter in 22nd place was *[The Lead with Jake Tapper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lead_with_Jake_Tapper)*, [CNN](62028.md)'s highest rated show.[^cite_note-145] According to a Fox News article on the subject, *Fox & Friends* averaged 1.8 million viewers, topping CNN's *[New Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Day_\(TV_series\))* and MSNBC's *[Morning Joe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morning_Joe)* combined. The same Fox News article said that the Fox Business Network also had its highest-rated quarter in history and that Fox News finished March as the highest-rated network in cable for the 45th consecutive month.[^cite_note-146]

According to the *[Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times)* on August 19, 2020: "Fox News Channel had six of last week's 11 highest-rated prime-time programs to finish first in the network ratings race for the third time since June" 2020.[^cite_note-147]

A [Morning Consult](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morning_Consult) survey the week after [Election Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Day_\(United_States\)) 2020 showed 30 percent of Republicans in the United States had an unfavorable opinion of Fox News, while 54 percent of Republicans viewed the network favorably, compared to 67 percent before the election. A [McClatchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McClatchy) news story suggested criticism from Donald Trump as a major reason, as well as the network's early calling of [Arizona](21883824.md) for [Joe Biden](145422.md), and later joining other networks in declaring Biden the winner of the [2020 election](48410011.md).[^cite_note-Herald-148]

Ratings were also down for Fox News. Although it remained ahead of other networks overall, its morning show fell out of first place for the first time since 2001. Trump recommended [OANN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OANN), which was gaining viewers. [Newsmax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newsmax) was also increasing in popularity.[^cite_note-Herald-148]

Following a decline in ratings after the 2020 U.S. presidential election, in 2021, Fox News regained its lead in cable news ratings ahead of CNN and MSNBC.[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150]

### Demographics

As indicated by a 2013 *New York Times* article, based on [Nielsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nielsen_ratings) statistics, Fox appears to have a mostly aged demographic.[^cite_note-151] In March 2024, Fox was the most watched news network in total day and prime time viewers in primetime, with 2.135 million/1.306 million viewers respectively, compared to MSNBC with A25-54 demo, 1.307 million in primetime and 830,000 in day viewers, and CNN with 601,000 in primetime and 462,000 in day viewers. In the Adults age 25-54 category, Fox also leads with 246,000 in primetime and 158,000 in day viewers, followed by MSNBC with 133,000 viewers in primetime and 86,000 viewers in day, and CNN with 124,000 viewers in primetime and 85,000 in day viewers.[^cite_note-152] According to the same Nielsen analysis, MSNBC is the second most watched news network.

In 2008, in the 25–54 age group, Fox News had an average of 557,000 viewers, but dropped to 379,000 in 2013 while increasing its overall audience from 1.89 million in 2010 to 2.02 million in 2013. The median age of a prime-time viewer was 68 as of 2015.[^cite_note-153] A 2019 [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) survey showed that among those who named Fox News as their main source for political news, 69% are aged 50 or older.[^cite_note-Grieco-2020-154]

According to a 2013 [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_\(company\)) poll, 94% of Fox viewers "either identify as or lean Republican".[^cite_note-155] The 2019 Pew survey showed that among people who named Fox News as their main source for political and election news, 93% identify as Republicans. Among the top eight political news sources named by at least 2% of American adults, the results show Fox News and MSNBC as the two news channels with the most partisan audiences.[^cite_note-Grieco-2020-154]

## Slogan

Fox News Channel originally used the slogan "Fair and Balanced", which was coined by network co-founder [Roger Ailes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Ailes) while the network was being established. *The New York Times* described the slogan as being a "blunt signal that Fox News planned to counteract what Mr. Ailes and many others viewed as a [liberal bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_bias) ingrained in television coverage by establishment news networks".[^cite_note-nyt-fairandbalanced-156][^cite_note-salon-fairandbalanced-157] In a 2013 interview with [Peter Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Robinson_\(speechwriter\)) of the [Hoover Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoover_Institution), [Rupert Murdoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Murdoch) defended the company's "Fair and Balanced" slogan, saying, "In fact, you'll find just as many Democrats as Republicans on and so on".[^cite_note-158]

In August 2003, [Fox News sued](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_v._Franken) comedian [Al Franken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Franken) over his use of the slogan as a subtitle for his book, *[Lies and the Lying Liars Who Tell Them: A Fair and Balanced Look at the Right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lies_and_the_Lying_Liars_Who_Tell_Them:_A_Fair_and_Balanced_Look_at_the_Right)*, which is critical of Fox News Channel.[^cite_note-foxsuit-159] The lawsuit was dropped three days later, after Judge [Denny Chin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denny_Chin) refused its request for an [injunction](39343.md). In his decision, Chin ruled the case was "wholly without merit, both factually and legally". He went on to suggest that Fox News' trademark on the phrase "fair and balanced" could be invalid.[^cite_note-foxloss-160] In December 2003, FNC won a legal battle concerning the slogan, when [AlterNet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AlterNet) filed a cancellation petition with the [United States Patent and Trademark Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Patent_and_Trademark_Office) (USPTO) to have FNC's trademark rescinded as inaccurate. AlterNet included [Robert Greenwald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Greenwald)'s documentary film *Outfoxed* (2004) as supporting evidence in its case.[^cite_note-coyle-161] After losing early motions, AlterNet withdrew its petition; the USPTO dismissed the case.[^cite_note-162] In 2008, FNC used the slogan "We Report, You Decide", referring to "You Decide 2008" (FNC's original slogan for its coverage of election issues).

In August 2016, Fox News Channel began to quietly phase out the "Fair and Balanced" slogan in favor of "Most Watched, Most Trusted"; when these changes were reported in June 2017 by [Gabriel Sherman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Sherman) (a writer who had written a biography on Ailes), a network executive said the change "has nothing to do with programming or editorial decisions". It was speculated by media outlets that Fox News Channel was wishing to distance itself from Ailes' tenure at the network.[^cite_note-nyt-fairandbalanced-156][^cite_note-salon-fairandbalanced-157][^cite_note-163]

In March 2018, the network introduced a new promotional campaign, "Real News. Real Honest Opinion", which highlighted personalities from its news- and opinion-based programming.[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166] The following year, Fox News Channel launched the campaign "America is Watching", which is used primarily to emphasize the network's viewership.[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168] In mid-November 2020, following the election, Fox News began to use the tagline "Standing Up For What's Right" to promote its primetime lineup.[^cite_note-169]

## Content

### Benghazi attack and aftermath

Further information: [2012 Benghazi attack](37018641.md)

Fox News provided extensive coverage of the [2012 Benghazi attack](37018641.md), which host [Sean Hannity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sean_Hannity) described in December 2012 as "the story that the mainstream media ignores" and "obviously, a cover-up. And we will get to the bottom of it."[^cite_note-170] Programming analysis by media watchdog [Media Matters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_Matters), which has declared a "War on Fox News", found that during the twenty months following the Benghazi attacks, FNC ran 1,098 segments on the issue, including:[^cite_note-171]

- 478 segments involving [Susan Rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susan_Rice)'s September 16, 2012, Sunday news show appearances, during which she was falsely accused of lying

- 382 segments on *[Special Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Report_\(TV_program\))*, the network's flagship news program

- 281 segments alleging a "cover-up" by the Obama administration

- 144 interviews of GOP members of Congress, but five interviews of Democratic members of Congress and Obama administration officials

- 120 comparisons to [Iran-Contra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran-Contra), [Watergate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watergate), and the actions of the Nixon administration

- 100 segments falsely suggesting the administration issued a "stand-down order" to prevent a rescue operation in Benghazi

Over nearly four years after the Benghazi attack, there were [ten official investigations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Investigation_into_the_2012_Benghazi_attack), including six by Republican-controlled House committees. None of the investigations found any evidence of scandal, cover-up or lying by Obama administration officials.

### Uranium One

Further information: [Uranium One controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium_One_controversy)

From 2015 into 2018, Fox News broadcast extensive coverage of an alleged scandal surrounding the sale of [Uranium One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium_One) to Russian interests, which host Sean Hannity characterized as "one of the biggest scandals in American history".[^cite_note-172] According to Media Matters, the Fox News coverage extended throughout the programming day, with particular emphasis by Hannity.[^cite_note-173] The network promoted an ultimately unfounded narrative asserting that, as Secretary of State, [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) personally approved the Uranium One sale in exchange for \$145 million in bribes paid to the [Clinton Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_Foundation). Donald Trump repeated these allegations as a candidate and as president.[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-WPFoxdebunk-175][^cite_note-176] No evidence of wrongdoing by Clinton had been found after four years of allegations, an FBI investigation, and the 2017 appointment of a Federal attorney to evaluate the investigation. In November 2017, Fox News host [Shepard Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shepard_Smith) concisely debunked the alleged scandal, infuriating viewers who suggested he should work for CNN or MSNBC.[^cite_note-WPFoxdebunk-175] Hannity later called Smith "clueless", while Smith stated: "I get it, that some of our opinion programming is there strictly to be entertaining. I get that. I don't work there. I wouldn't work there."[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178]

### Pro-Republican and pro-Trump bias



Fox News has been described as broadcasting content designed to provoke negative emotional responses that align with political or other narratives.

[^cite_note-Oxford_20251201-179]

[^cite_note-TobinSmith-180]

[^cite_note-Feldman_summary-181]

[^cite_note-Salon_20231203-182]

Fox News has been described as conservative media,[^cite_note-DellaVigna-2007-183][^cite_note-Azari-2016-184] and as providing [biased reporting](18932.md) in favor of conservative political positions,[^cite_note-Berry_2014-34][^cite_note-185][^cite_note-Rydgren-2018-186] the Republican Party,[^cite_note-LaMonica2009-187][^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189] and President Donald Trump.[^cite_note-Kludt-2018-37][^cite_note-Schwartz-2017b-38][^cite_note-Benkler_08232020-105] Political scientist Jonathan Bernstein described Fox News as an expanded part of the Republican Party.[^cite_note-Grossman-2016-27] Political scientists Matt Grossmann and David A. Hopkins wrote that Fox News helped "Republicans communicate with their base and spread their ideas, and they have been effective in mobilizing voters to participate in midterm elections (as in 2010 and 2014)."[^cite_note-Grossman-2016-27] Prior to 2000, Fox News lacked an ideological tilt, and had more Democrats watch the channel than Republicans.[^cite_note-190] During the [2004 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_United_States_presidential_election), Fox News was markedly more hostile in its coverage of Democratic presidential nominee [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry), and distinguished itself among cable news outlets for heavy coverage of the [Swift Boat smear campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiftboating) against Kerry.[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193] During President Obama's first term in office, Fox News helped launch and amplify the [Tea Party movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea_Party_movement), a conservative movement within the Republican Party that organized protests against Obama and his policies.[^cite_note-199] In the 2004 documentary *[Outfoxed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outfoxed)*, four people identified as former employees said that Fox News made them "slant the news in favor of conservatives".[^cite_note-Memmott-200] Fox News said that the film misrepresented the employment of these employees.[^cite_note-foxr1-201]

During the [Republican primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries), Fox News was perceived as trying to prevent Trump from clinching the nomination.[^cite_note-Azari-2016-184] Under Trump's presidency, Fox News remade itself into his image, as hardly any criticism of Trump could be heard on Fox News' prime-time shows.[^cite_note-Schwartz-2017b-38][^cite_note-Schwartz-2017-202] In Fox News' news reporting, the network dedicated far more coverage to Hillary Clinton-related stories, which critics argued was intended to deflect attention from the investigation into [Russian interference in the 2016 United States elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_interference_in_the_2016_United_States_elections).[^cite_note-Schwartz-2017-202] Trump provided significant access to Fox News during his presidency, giving 19 interviews to the channel while only 6 in total to other news channels by November 2017; *The New York Times* described Trump's Fox News interviews as "softball interviews" and some of the interviewers' interview styles as "fawning".[^cite_note-203] In July 2018, *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* has described the network's coverage of Trump's presidency as "reliably fawning".[^cite_note-204] From 2015 to 2017, the Fox News prime-time lineup changed from being skeptical and questioning of Trump to a "Trump safe space, with a dose of [Bannonist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bannonist) populism once considered on the fringe".[^cite_note-Grynbaum-2017b-205] The Fox News website has also become more extreme in its rhetoric since Trump's election; according to [Columbia University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_University)'s Tow Center for Digital Journalism, the Fox News website has "gone a little *[Breitbart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breitbart)*" over time.[^cite_note-206] At the start of 2018, Fox News mostly ignored high-profile scandals in the Trump administration which received ample coverage in other national media outlets, such as White House Staff Secretary [Rob Porter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rob_Porter)'s resignation amid domestic abuse allegations, the downgrading of [Jared Kushner](6191053.md)'s security clearance, and the existence of a [non-disclosure agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-disclosure_agreement) between Trump and the porn star [Stormy Daniels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stormy_Daniels).[^cite_note-207]

In March 2019, [Jane Mayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jane_Mayer) reported in *[The New Yorker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Yorker)* that Fox News.com reporter [Diana Falzone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_Falzone) had the story of the [Stormy Daniels–Donald Trump scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stormy_Daniels%E2%80%93Donald_Trump_scandal) before the 2016 election, but that Fox News executive [Ken LaCorte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_LaCorte) told her: "Good reporting, kiddo. But Rupert \[Murdoch] wants Donald Trump to win. So just let it go." The story was killed; LaCorte denied making the statement to Falzone, but conceded: "I was the person who made the call. I didn't run it upstairs to Roger Ailes or others. ... I didn't do it to protect Donald Trump." She added that "\[Falzone] had put up a story that just wasn't anywhere close to being something I was comfortable publishing." [Nik Richie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nik_Richie), who claimed to be one of the sources for the story, called LaCorte's account "complete bullshit", adding that "Fox News was culpable. I voted for Trump, and I like Fox, but they did their own '[catch and kill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catch_and_kill)' on the story to protect him."[^cite_note-auto1-208][^cite_note-209]

A 2008 study found Fox News gave disproportionate attention to polls suggesting low approval for President [Bill Clinton](3356.md).[^cite_note-210] A 2009 study found Fox News was less likely to pick up stories that reflected well on Democrats, and more likely to pick up stories that reflected well on Republicans.[^cite_note-211] A 2010 study comparing Fox News Channel's *Special Report With Brit Hume* and NBC's *[Nightly News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC_Nightly_News)* coverage of the wars in Iraq and Afghanistan during 2005 concluded "Fox News was much more sympathetic to the administration than NBC", suggesting "if scholars continue to find evidence of a partisan or ideological bias at FNC ... they should consider Fox as alternative, rather than mainstream, media".[^cite_note-212]

Research finds that Fox News increases Republican vote shares and makes Republican politicians more partisan.[^cite_note-Martin-2017-31][^cite_note-Clinton-2014-32][^cite_note-Schroeder-2015-33] A 2007 study, using the introduction of Fox News into local markets (1996–2000) as an instrumental variable, found that in the [2000 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_presidential_election) "Republicans gained 0.4 to 0.7 percentage points in the towns that broadcast Fox News", suggesting "Fox News convinced 3 to 28 percent of its viewers to vote Republican, depending on the audience measure".[^cite_note-DellaVigna-2007-183] These results were confirmed by a 2015 study.[^cite_note-Schroeder-2015-33] A 2014 study, using the same instrumental variable, found congressional "representatives become less supportive of President Clinton in districts where Fox News begins broadcasting than similar representatives in similar districts where Fox News was not broadcast."[^cite_note-Clinton-2014-32] Another 2014 paper found Fox News viewing increased Republican vote shares among voters who identified as Republican or independent.[^cite_note-213] A 2017 study, using channel positions as an instrumental variable, found "Fox News increases Republican vote shares by 0.3 points among viewers induced into watching 2.5 additional minutes per week by variation in position."[^cite_note-Martin-2017-31] This study used a different methodology for a later period and found an ever bigger effect and impact, leading [Matthew Yglesias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_Yglesias) to write in the *[Political Communication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_Communication_\(journal\))* academic journal that they "suggest that conventional wisdom may be greatly underestimating the significance of Fox as a factor in American politics."[^cite_note-214]

Fox News publicly denies it is biased,[^cite_note-215] with Murdoch and Ailes saying to have included Murdoch's statement that Fox has "given room to both sides, whereas only one side had it before".[^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217] In June 2009,[^cite_note-218] Fox News host [Chris Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Wallace) said: "I think we are the counter-weight \[to NBC News] ... they have a liberal agenda, and we tell the other side of the story."[^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221] In 2004, [Robert Greenwald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Greenwald)'s documentary film *Outfoxed: Rupert Murdoch's War on Journalism* argued Fox News had a conservative bias and featured clips from Fox News and internal memos from editorial vice president [John Moody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Moody_\(journalist\)) directing Fox News staff on how to report certain subjects.[^cite_note-tilting-222][^cite_note-ChristiansFackler2015-223] Fox News' most popular programs such as Sean Hannity and Tucker Carlson do not make any claims to be accurate or fact-checked, and have little to no distinction between news and commentary.[^cite_note-Austin_2021-106]

A leaked memo from Fox News vice president [Bill Sammon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Sammon) to news staff at the height of the [health care reform in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_reform_in_the_United_States) debate has been cited as an example of the pro-Republican bias of Fox News. His memo asked the staff to "use the term 'government-run health insurance,' or, when brevity is a concern, 'government option,' whenever possible". The memo was sent shortly after Republican pollster [Frank Luntz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Luntz) advised [Sean Hannity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sean_Hannity) on his Fox show: "If you call it a public option, the American people are split. If you call it the government option, the public is overwhelmingly against it."[^cite_note-224]

Surveys suggest Fox News is widely perceived to be ideological. A 2009 Pew survey found Fox News is viewed as the most ideological channel in America, with 47 percent of those surveyed said Fox News is "mostly conservative", 14 percent said "mostly liberal" and 24 percent said "neither". In comparison, MSNBC had 36 percent identify it as "mostly liberal", 11 percent as "mostly conservative" and 27 percent as "neither". CNN had 37 percent describe it as "mostly liberal", 11 percent as "mostly conservative" and 33 percent as "neither".[^cite_note-225] A 2004 [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) survey found FNC was cited (unprompted) by 69 percent of national journalists as a conservative news organization.[^cite_note-226] A [Rasmussen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rasmussen_Reports) poll found 31 percent of Americans felt Fox News had a conservative bias, and 15 percent that it had a liberal bias. It found 36 percent believed Fox News delivers news with neither a conservative or liberal bias, compared with 37 percent who said [NPR](29697232.md) delivers news with no conservative or liberal bias and 32 percent who said the same of CNN.[^cite_note-227]

[David Carr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Carr_\(journalist\)), media critic for *[The New York Times](30680.md)*, praised the [2012 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_United_States_presidential_election) results coverage on Fox News for the network's response to Republican adviser and Fox News contributor [Karl Rove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Rove) challenging its call that [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) would win Ohio and the election. Fox's prediction was correct. Carr wrote: "Over many months, Fox lulled its conservative base with agitprop: that President Obama was a clear failure, that a majority of Americans saw [\[Mitt\] Romney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitt_Romney) as a good alternative in hard times, and that polls showing otherwise were politically motivated and not to be believed. But on Tuesday night, the people in charge of Fox News were confronted with a stark choice after it became clear that Mr. Romney had fallen short: was Fox, first and foremost, a place for advocacy or a place for news? In this moment, at least, Fox chose news."[^cite_note-228]

A May 2017 study conducted by [Harvard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_University)'s Shorenstein Center on Media, Politics and Public Policy examined coverage of Trump's [first 100 days](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_100_days_of_the_first_Donald_Trump_presidency) in office by several major mainstream media outlets including Fox.[^cite_note-229] It found Trump received 80% negative coverage from the overall media, and received the least negative coverage on Fox – 52% negative and 48% positive.[^cite_note-230]

On March 14, 2017, [Andrew Napolitano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Napolitano), a Fox News commentator, claimed on *Fox & Friends* that British intelligence agency [GCHQ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GCHQ) had wiretapped Trump on behalf of Barack Obama during the [2016 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-Grynbaum-2017-231][^cite_note-232] On March 16, 2017, White House spokesman [Sean Spicer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sean_Spicer) repeated the claim.[^cite_note-Grynbaum-2017-231] When Trump was questioned about the claim at a news conference, he said "All we did was quote a certain very talented legal mind who was the one responsible for saying that on television. I didn't make an opinion on it."[^cite_note-Baker-2017-233] On March 17, 2017, [Shepard Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shepard_Smith), a Fox News anchor, admitted the network had no evidence that Trump was under surveillance. British officials said the White House was backing off the claim.[^cite_note-Baker-2017-233] Napolitano was later suspended by Fox News for making the claim.[^cite_note-234]

In June 2018, Fox News executives instructed producers to head off inappropriate remarks made on the shows aired by the network by hosts and commentators.[^cite_note-Schwartz-2018-235] The instructions came after a number of Fox News hosts and guests made incendiary comments about [the Trump administration's policy of separating migrant children from their parents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_administration_family_separation_policy).[^cite_note-Schwartz-2018-235] Fox News host Laura Ingraham had likened the child detention centers that the children were in to "summer camps". Guest [Corey Lewandowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corey_Lewandowski) mocked the story of a 10-year-old child with [Down syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Down_syndrome) being separated from her mother; the Fox News host did not address Lewandowski's statement.[^cite_note-Schwartz-2018-235] Guest [Ann Coulter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ann_Coulter) falsely claimed that the separated children were "child actors"; the Fox News host did not challenge her claim.[^cite_note-Schwartz-2018-235] In a segment on Trump's alleged use of racial dog whistles, one Fox News contributor told an African-American whom he was debating: "You're out of your cotton-picking mind."[^cite_note-Schwartz-2018-235]

According to the 2016 book *Asymmetric Politics* by political scientists Matt Grossmann and David A. Hopkins, "Fox News tends to raise the profile of scandals and controversies involving Democrats that receive scant attention in other media, such as the [relationship between Barack Obama and William Ayers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Ayers_2008_presidential_election_controversy) ... Hillary Clinton's role in the fatal 2012 attacks on the American consulate in Benghazi, Libya; the [gun-running scandal known as 'Fast and Furious'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ATF_gunwalking_scandal); the business practices of federal loan guarantee recipient [Solyndra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solyndra); the past activism of Obama White House operative [Van Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Van_Jones); the 2004 attacks on John Kerry by the [Swift Boat Veterans for Truth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swift_Boat_Veterans_for_Truth); the [controversial sermons of Obama's Chicago pastor Jeremiah Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremiah_Wright_controversy); the [filming of undercover videos of supposed wrongdoing by the liberal activist group ACORN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACORN_2009_undercover_videos_controversy); and the 'war on Christmas' supposedly waged every December by secular, multicultural liberals."[^cite_note-Grossman-2016b-195]

In October 2018, Fox News ran laudatory coverage of a meeting between Trump-supporting rapper [Kanye West](523032.md) and President Trump in the [Oval Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oval_Office). Fox News had previously run negative coverage of rappers and their involvement with Democratic politicians and causes, such as when Fox News ran headlines describing conscious hip-hop artist [Common](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_\(rapper\)) as "vile" and a "cop-killer rapper", and when Fox News ran negative coverage of Kanye West before he became a Trump supporter.[^cite_note-236]

On November 4, 2018, Trump's website, DonaldJTrump.com, announced in a press release that Fox News host Sean Hannity would make a "special guest appearance" with Trump at a midterm campaign rally the following night in [Cape Girardeau, Missouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Girardeau,_Missouri).[^cite_note-auto-237] The following morning, Hannity tweeted "To be clear, I will not be on stage campaigning with the President."[^cite_note-238] Hannity appeared at the president's lectern on stage at the rally, immediately mocking the "fake news" at the back of the auditorium, Fox News reporters among them. Several Fox News employees expressed outrage at Hannity's actions, with one stating that "a new line was crossed".[^cite_note-239] Hannity later asserted that his action was not pre-planned, and Fox News stated it "does not condone any talent participating in campaign events".[^cite_note-240] Fox News host [Jeanine Pirro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeanine_Pirro) also appeared on stage with Trump at the rally. The Trump press release was later removed from Trump's website.[^cite_note-auto-237][^cite_note-241]

Fox News released a poll of registered voters, jointly conducted by two polling organizations, on June 16, 2019. The poll found some unfavorable results for Trump, including a record high 50% thought the Trump campaign had coordinated with the Russian government, and 50% thought he should be impeached – 43% saying he should also be removed from office – while 48% said they did not favor impeachment.[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243] The next morning on *[Fox & Friends First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_&_Friends_First)*, host [Heather Childers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heather_Childers) twice misrepresented the poll results, stating "a new Fox News poll shows most voters don't want impeachment" and "at least half of U.S. voters do not think President Trump should be impeached," while the on-screen display of the actual poll question was also incorrect. Later that morning on *[America's Newsroom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America's_Newsroom)*, the on-screen display showed the correct poll question and results, but highlighted the 48% of respondents who opposed impeachment rather than the 50% who supported it (the latter being broken-out into two figures). As host [Bill Hemmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Hemmer) drew guest [Byron York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byron_York)'s attention to the 48% opposed figure, they did not discuss the 50% support figure, while the on-screen [chyron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_third) read: "Fox News Poll: 43% Support Trump's Impeachment and Removal, 48% Oppose."[^cite_note-244] Later that day, Trump tweeted: "@FoxNews Polls are always bad for me...Something weird going on at Fox."[^cite_note-245]

In April 2017, it became known that former Obama administration national security advisor [Susan Rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susan_Rice) sought the [unmasking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unmasking_by_U.S._intelligence_agencies) of Trump associates who were unidentified in intelligence reports, notably Trump's incoming national security advisor [Michael Flynn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Flynn), during the [presidential transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_presidential_transition_of_Donald_Trump).[^cite_note-246] In May 2020, acting [Director of National Intelligence](456082.md) [Richard Grenell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Grenell), a Trump loyalist, declassified a list of Obama administration officials who had also requested unmasking of Trump associates, which was subsequently publicly released by Republican senators.[^cite_note-247] That month, attorney general [Bill Barr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Barr) appointed federal prosecutor [John Bash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Bash) to examine the unmaskings.[^cite_note-248] Fox News primetime hosts declared the unmaskings a "domestic spying operation" for which the Obama administration was "exposed" in the "biggest abuse of power" in American history.[^cite_note-249] The Bash inquiry closed months later with no findings of substantive wrongdoing.[^cite_note-250]

However, certain Fox personalities have not had as much of a favorable reception from Trump: news anchors [Shepard Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shepard_Smith) (who retired from Fox in 2019) and Chris Wallace have been criticized by Trump for allegedly being adversarial,[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252] alongside Fox analyst [Andrew Napolitano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Napolitano), who said Trump's actions in the [Trump–Ukraine scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump%E2%80%93Ukraine_scandal) were "both criminal and impeachable behavior".[^cite_note-253] Trump was also critical of the network hiring former [DNC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_National_Committee) chair [Donna Brazile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donna_Brazile), in 2019.[^cite_note-254] The relationship between Trump and Fox News, as well as other [Rupert Murdoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Murdoch)-controlled outlets, soured following the [2020 United States presidential election](48410011.md), as Trump refused to concede that [Joe Biden](145422.md) had been elected [President-elect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President-elect_of_the_United_States).[^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256] This negative tonal shift led to increased viewership of Newsmax and One America News among Trump and his supporters due to their increased antipathy towards Fox;[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260] and as a result, Fox released promotional videos of their opinion hosts disputing the election results, promoting a Trump-affiliated [conspiracy theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory) about [voter fraud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_fraud).[^cite_note-261] By one measure, Newsmax saw a 497% spike in viewership, while Fox News saw a 38% decline.[^cite_note-262]

Writing for the [Poynter Institute for Media Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poynter_Institute_for_Media_Studies) in February 2021, senior media writer Tom Jones argued that the primary distinction between Fox News and MSNBC is not right bias vs. left bias, but rather that much of the content on Fox News, especially during its primetime programs, "is not based in truth".[^cite_note-263]

The *[Tampa Bay Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampa_Bay_Times)* reported in August 2021 that it had reviewed four months of emails indicating Fox News producers had coordinated with aides of Florida governor [Ron DeSantis](36729527.md) to promote his political prospects by inviting him for frequent network appearances, exchanging talking points and, in one case, helping him to stage an exclusive news event.[^cite_note-264]

In February 2024, Alan Rosenblatt of [Johns Hopkins University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johns_Hopkins_University) said that Fox News "is an entertainment company that has a news division, not a news company", adding that it "not only does not provide that distinction, it goes out of its way to make it difficult to see the difference. They make their opinion programs look like news programs, and they incorporate enough opinion content on their news programs to further that deception."[^cite_note-\:0-43]

In early 2024, Fox News host Jesse Watters promoted a conspiracy theory involving [Taylor Swift](5422144.md), [Travis Kelce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travis_Kelce), and the Democratic Party in hopes of influencing voters ahead of the [U.S. presidential primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_primary) season.[^cite_note-Darcy_2024-42][^cite_note-\:0-43]

Fox News has published headlines accusing the [English Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Wikipedia) of having a left-wing and [socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist) bias.[^cite_note-265]

### Coverage of Russia investigation

Further information: [Mueller special counsel investigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mueller_special_counsel_investigation)

On October 30, 2017, when special counsel [Robert Mueller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Mueller) indicted [Paul Manafort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Manafort) and [Rick Gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rick_Gates_\(political_consultant\)), and revealed [George Papadopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Papadopoulos) had pleaded guilty (all of whom were involved in the Trump 2016 campaign), this was the focus of most media's coverage, except Fox News'.[^cite_note-Rosenberg-2017-266] Hosts and guests on Fox News called for Mueller to be fired.[^cite_note-Rosenberg-2017-266][^cite_note-267] Sean Hannity and Tucker Carlson focused their shows on unsubstantiated allegations that Clinton sold uranium to Russia in exchange for donations to the Clinton Foundation and on the Clinton campaign's role in funding the [Steele dossier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steele_dossier).[^cite_note-Rosenberg-2017-266][^cite_note-Kludt-2017-268] Hannity asserted: "The very thing they are accusing President Trump of doing, they did it themselves."[^cite_note-Rosenberg-2017-266] During the segment, Hannity mistakenly referred to Clinton as President Clinton.[^cite_note-Rosenberg-2017-266][^cite_note-269] Fox News dedicated extensive coverage to the uranium story, which Democrats said was an attempt to distract from Mueller's intensifying investigation.[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271] CNN described the coverage as "a tour de force in deflection and dismissal".[^cite_note-Kludt-2017-268] On October 31, CNN reported Fox News employees were dissatisfied with their outlet's coverage of the Russia investigation, with employees calling it an "embarrassment", "laughable", and saying it "does the viewer a huge disservice and further divides the country" and that it is "another blow to journalists at Fox who come in every day wanting to cover the news in a fair and objective way".[^cite_note-272][^cite_note-273]

When the investigation by special counsel Robert Mueller into [Russian interference in the 2016 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_interference_in_the_2016_presidential_election) intensified in October 2017, the focus of Fox News coverage turned "what they see as the scandal and wrongdoing of President Trump's political opponents. In reports like these, Bill and Hillary Clinton are prominent and recurring characters because they are considered the real conspirators working with the Russians to undermine American democracy."[^cite_note-274] Paul Waldman of *The Washington Post* described the coverage as "No puppet. You're the puppet", saying it was a "careful, coordinated, and comprehensive strategy" to distract from Mueller's investigation.[^cite_note-275] German Lopes of *[Vox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vox_\(website\))* said Fox News' coverage has reached "levels of self-parody" as it dedicated coverage to low-key stories, such as a controversial *Newsweek* op-ed and hamburger emojis, while other networks had wall-to-wall coverage of Mueller's indictments.[^cite_note-276]

A *[FiveThirtyEight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FiveThirtyEight)* analysis of Russia-related media coverage in cable news found most mentions of Russia on Fox News were spoken in close proximity to "uranium" and "dossier".[^cite_note-277] On November 1, 2017, *Vox* analyzed the transcripts of Fox News, CNN and MSNBC, and found Fox News "was unable to talk about the Mueller investigation without bringing up Hillary Clinton", "talked significantly less about George Papadopoulos—the Trump campaign adviser whose plea deal with Mueller provides the most explicit evidence thus far that the campaign knew of the Russian government's efforts to help Trump—than its competitors", and "repeatedly called Mueller's credibility into question".[^cite_note-278]

In December 2017, Fox News escalated its attacks on the Mueller investigation, with hosts and guest commentators suggesting the investigation amounted to a [coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coup).[^cite_note-Vernon-2017-279][^cite_note-Schmidt-2017-280][^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282][^cite_note-283] Guest co-host Kevin Jackson referred to a right-wing conspiracy theory claiming Strzok's messages are evidence of a plot by FBI agents to assassinate Trump, a claim which the other Fox co-hosts quickly said is not supported by any credible evidence.[^cite_note-284][^cite_note-285] Fox News host Jeanine Pirro called the Mueller investigation team a "criminal cabal" and said the team ought to be arrested.[^cite_note-Vernon-2017-279] Other Fox News figures referred to the investigation as "corrupt", "crooked", and "illegitimate", and likened the FBI to the KGB, the Soviet-era spy organization that routinely tortured and summarily executed people.[^cite_note-Schmidt-2017-280] Political scientists and scholars of coups described the Fox News rhetoric as scary and dangerous.[^cite_note-Schmidt-2017-280] Experts on coups rejected that the Mueller investigation amounted to a coup; rather, the Fox News rhetoric was dangerous to democracy and mirrored the kind of rhetoric that occurs before [purges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purge).[^cite_note-Schmidt-2017-280] A number of observers argued the Fox News rhetoric was intended to discredit the Mueller investigation and sway President Donald Trump to fire Mueller.[^cite_note-286]

In August 2018, Fox News was criticized for giving more prominent coverage of a murder committed by an undocumented immigrant than the convictions of Donald Trump's former campaign manager, Paul Manafort, and his long-term personal attorney, [Michael Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Cohen_\(lawyer\)).[^cite_note-Relman-2018-287] At the same time, most other national mainstream media gave wall-to-wall coverage of the convictions.[^cite_note-288] Fox News hosts Dana Perrino and Jason Chaffetz argued that voters care far more about the murder than the convictions of the President's former top aides, and hosts Tucker Carlson and Sean Hannity downplayed the convictions.[^cite_note-289][^cite_note-Relman-2018-287][^cite_note-290]

### False claims about other media

#### CNN's Jake Tapper

In November 2017, following the [2017 New York City truck attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_New_York_City_truck_attack) wherein a terrorist shouted "Allahu Akbar", Fox News distorted a statement by [Jake Tapper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jake_Tapper) to make it appear as if Tapper had said "[Allahu Akbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allahu_Akbar)" can be used under the most "beautiful circumstances". Fox News omitted that Tapper had said the use of "Allahu Akbar" in the terrorist attack was not one of these beautiful circumstances. A headline on FoxNews.com was preceded by a tag reading "OUTRAGEOUS". The Fox News Twitter account distorted the statement further, saying "Jake Tapper Says 'Allahu Akbar' Is 'Beautiful' Right After NYC Terror Attack" in a tweet that was later deleted. Tapper chastised Fox News for choosing to "deliberately lie" and said "there was a time when one could tell the difference between Fox and the nutjobs at *[Infowars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infowars)*. It's getting tougher and tougher. Lies are lies."[^cite_note-Abadi-2017-291] In 2009, Tapper had come to the defense of Fox News while he was a White House correspondent for ABC News, after the [Obama administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obama_administration) claimed that the network was not a legitimate news organization.[^cite_note-Tornoe-2017-292]

Fox News guest host [Jason Chaffetz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jason_Chaffetz) apologized to Tapper for misrepresenting his statement.[^cite_note-Balluck-2017-293] After Fox News had deleted the tweet, Sean Hannity repeated the misrepresentation and called Tapper "liberal fake news CNN's fake Jake Tapper" and mocked his ratings.[^cite_note-Tornoe-2017-292][^cite_note-Balluck-2017-293]

#### *The New York Times*

In July 2017, a report by *Fox & Friends* falsely said *The New York Times* had disclosed intelligence in one of its stories and that this intelligence disclosure helped [Abu Bakr al-Baghdadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Bakr_al-Baghdadi), the leader of the [Islamic State](9087364.md), to evade capture.[^cite_note-Gordon-2017-294][^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296] The report cited an inaccurate assertion by Gen. [Tony Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raymond_A._Thomas), the head of the [United States Special Operations Command](45204749.md), that a major newspaper had disclosed the intelligence.[^cite_note-Gordon-2017-294][^cite_note-Bromwich-2017a-297] Fox News said it was *The New York Times*, repeatedly running the chyron "NYT Foils U.S. Attempt To Take Out Al-Bahgdadi".[^cite_note-Bromwich-2017a-297] [Pete Hegseth](34076202.md), one of the show's hosts, criticized the "failing *New York Times*".[^cite_note-Bromwich-2017a-297] President Donald Trump tweeted about the *Fox & Friends* report shortly after it first aired, saying "The Failing *New York Times* foiled U.S. attempt to kill the single most wanted terrorist, Al-Baghdadi. Their sick agenda over National Security."[^cite_note-Gordon-2017-294] Fox News later updated the story, but without apologizing to *The New York Times* or responding directly to the inaccuracies.[^cite_note-Bromwich-2017a-297]
